//角落書屋/Slices/Compounds
"骸骨筑入墙内"	hái gǔ zhù rù qiáng nèi	"bodies buried inside the wall" — Folk tradition since at least Tang times has held that the bones of dead labourers were mixed into the rammed earth. Archaeology has confirmed nearby labourer cemeteries with graves of those who died building the wall, but no systematic evidence that bodies were used as construction material.
"可以从太空看见"	kě yǐ cóng tài kōng kàn jiàn	"visible from space" — The wall cannot be seen from space with the naked eye. It is long but only four to eight metres wide, narrower than most highways. The claim was a 1932 invention from Ripley's Believe It or Not! — published before any human had reached orbit.
"秦始皇修了长城"	qín shǐ huáng xiū le cháng chéng	"Qin Shihuang built the Great Wall" — He connected and extended pre-existing Warring States walls. He did not build from nothing, and what he did build is mostly gone. The visual wall most people picture is Ming, fifteen centuries later.
"一道连续的墙"	yī dào lián xù de qiáng	"a continuous wall" — The Great Wall is not, and never was, a single continuous line. Even the Ming wall has parallel branches, doubled-back sections, gaps where rivers and mountains made building unnecessary, and stretches where it thins to a chain of isolated beacon towers.
*把书看	* bǎ shū kàn	WRONG — verb must have a resultative element — The most fundamental 把 error: using a bare verb with no resultative or directional complement. The construction requires that the action have a clear outcome. The sentence is ungrammatical because it doesn't tell us what happened to the book as a result.
*把他喜欢	* bǎ tā xǐ huān	WRONG — stative/emotional verbs cannot take 把 — Stative verbs (是, 有, 像), mental-state verbs (喜欢, 爱, 恨, 讨厌, 知道, 认识, 觉得), and perception verbs (看见, 听见, 闻到) cannot be used in the 把 construction. The 把 construction requires an agent who deliberately acts on a concrete object with a physical or tangible result.
*把一本书看完	* bǎ yī běn shū kàn wán	WRONG (usually) — object must be definite — The object of 把 must be definite , a specific, known thing. An indefinite object (a book you haven't mentioned before, an unspecified amount) generally cannot be used. The construction implies you are taking hold of a particular thing and doing something to it.
*我把他看见了	* wǒ bǎ tā kàn jian le	WRONG — unaffected objects cannot take 把 — The object of the 把 construction must be affected by the action , it must undergo a change, be moved, be consumed, be transformed, or be disposed of. Perception and cognition verbs (看见 see, 听见 hear, 闻到 smell) involve objects that are registered but not physically affected.
阿弥陀经	Ā mí tuó Jīng	Smaller Sukhāvatīvyūha Sutra — the daily liturgy sutra — Also called the 小经 Xiǎo Jīng (Small Sutra). The briefest of the three, translated by Kumārajīva in 402 CE in a Chinese so elegant that it became the definitive liturgical text.
阿是穴	ā shì xu é	Ashi points — Ashi points: tender spots with no fixed location, needled wherever the patient says "Ah, yes, that is it!" when pressed. Not on any meridian map, but recognized in classical texts as clinically valid.
阿姨	ā yí	aunt; older woman — Auntie , used to address unfamiliar middle-aged women in informal contexts: cleaning staff, market vendors, neighbors. More personal than 女士 and warmer than nothing.
啊	a	emotional / exclamatory particle
矮	ǎi	short (in height); squat
艾草	ài cǎo	mugwort; wormwood (Artemisia argyi) — 艾 (ài, mugwort) + 草 (cǎo, herb, grass). Hung over doorways on the Double Fifth to repel evil and insects, owing to its strong fragrance. Also used in traditional Chinese medicine (艾灸 àijiǔ, moxibustion) and in cooking (青团 qīngtuán, green rice cakes flavored with mugwort, eaten at Qīngmíng).
艾灸	ài jiǔ	Moxibustion , burning dried mugwort at or near acupoints to warm and tonify. Paired with needling in the term 针灸 (zhēnjiǔ), which names the two techniques together. Used specifically for cold-type and deficiency-type conditions where warming stimulation is indicated.
爱国	ài guó	patriotic; to love one's country — 爱 ài (love) + 国 guó (country). Patriotism. A deeply freighted term in Chinese political culture: 爱国主义 àiguó zhǔyì is patriotism as ideology, 爱国人士 patriotic personages. In everyday use, simply "to love one's country."
爱好	ài hào	hobby; interest; pastime — 爱 ài (to love) + 好 hào (to be fond of). A thing one loves doing , a hobby or passion. One of the first personal vocabulary items learners need: 你有什么爱好？(What are your hobbies?)
爱护	ài hù	to cherish and protect; to take loving care of — 爱 ài + 护 hù (to protect; to guard). To love something by protecting it. Used for public property (爱护环境), younger people (爱护学生), and cultural heritage. A civic and ethical register.
爱面子	ài miàn zi	to be very concerned with face; to care about appearances — 爱 (to love; to value; to be attached to). 爱面子 describes someone who places unusually high value on face , so much so that their behavior is shaped by the desire to maintain appearances even when it conflicts with practical sense.
爱情	ài qíng	romantic love; love as a feeling — 爱 ài + 情 qíng (feeling; sentiment; romance). The noun for romantic love as an abstract force or state. 爱情故事 = love story; 爱情电影 = romance film; 谈恋爱 is the verb for being in a relationship, but 爱情 names the thing itself.
安定	ān dìng	settled; secure; to settle (a society, a life) — 安 ān (peace; safe) + 定 dìng (settled). A secure, untroubled state of life or society, with the calm of 安 added to the fixity of 定: 安定的生活 "a settled life," 安定团结 "stability and unity."
安静	ān jìng	quiet
安全	ān quán	safe; safety
安全第一	ān quán dì yī	safety first; safety above all — 安全 ānquán (safety) + 第一 dì-yī (first). A fixed slogan ranking safety as the top priority. Here 第一 means "the foremost concern," not a numbered position.
安危	ān wēi	safety and danger; well-being — 安 (safety) bound to 危 (danger) as one word: the whole question of whether someone is safe or in peril. A fixed pair where the two opposites name a single concern, often someone's fate or welfare.
鹌鹑蛋	ān chún dàn	quail egg
案件	àn jiàn	a legal case; an incident under investigation — 案 àn (case; record; desk on which records were kept) + 件 jiàn (a piece). A discrete legal or police matter, treated as a single file of business. 案 alone meant the official desk and then the file of papers on it; with 件 it becomes one such case, counted and pursued on its own.
暗	àn	dim; dark; hidden
暗示	àn shì	to hint; to imply
昂首	áng shǒu	to hold the head high; head raised — 昂 áng (to raise, to lift up) + 首 shǒu (head). To carry the head high, in pride or defiance. 昂首挺胸 "head high and chest out" is the stock image of someone walking with proud, confident bearing. Literary in tone; the spoken equivalent uses 头 tóu.
傲慢	ào màn	arrogant; haughty; condescending — 傲 ào (proud, lofty, unbending) + 慢 màn (slow to respond, dismissive of others). The most common Chinese word for arrogance: deliberate condescension, the attitude of someone who considers themselves above the normal courtesies. Clearly pejorative.
八成	bā chéng	eighty percent; most likely; probably — 八 bā + 成 chéng (a tenth, a tenth part). Eight tenths, eighty percent. Literally a proportion (八成新, eighty percent new, that is, in good used condition), but very commonly an adverb of high probability: 八成是他, "it's most likely him," "I'd say eighty percent it's him." A natural everyday way to express…
八大菜系	bā dà cài xì	the Eight Great Cuisines — every regional banquet runs on baijiu
八股文	bā gǔ wén	the eight-legged essay — the rigid Ming-Qing examination form — The prescribed essay format mandated by the Ming dynasty (from 1487 onward) and retained through the Qing: eight sections in a fixed sequence, strict parallelism requirements, a prescribed length, and no deviation from the topic as given.
八卦	bā guà	the eight trigrams; gossip — 八 bā + 卦 guà (trigram; divinatory figure). The eight three-line figures of yin and yang at the foundation of the Yijing, used in divination, feng shui, and cosmology. In colloquial modern usage the same word means gossip: 八卦新闻 (tabloid news), 别八卦了 (stop gossiping).
八卦镜	bā guà jìng	The ba-gua mirror , an octagonal mirror surrounded by the eight trigrams (八卦) of the I Ching, hung above doorways to deflect negative energy or bad feng shui directed at the entrance. The mirror reflects harmful influences back toward their source.
八旗	bā qí	Eight Banners (Manchu military system)
八仙	bā xiān	the Eight Immortals — 八 bā + 仙 xiān (immortal; transcendent). The Eight Immortals of Daoist legend, a band of figures each with a distinctive emblem and power, including Lü Dongbin, He Xiangu, and Iron-Crutch Li.
八月	bā yu è	August; the eighth month — 八 bā + 月 yuè (month). August in the solar calendar. The fifteenth day of the eighth lunar month is the Mid-Autumn Festival (中秋节), one of the most important holidays of the year, when families gather to admire the full moon and eat mooncakes.
八字	bā zì	birth horoscope (Four Pillars)
拔罐	bá guàn	cupping therapy — 拔 bá (to pull; to extract) + 罐 guàn (jar; can). Cupping: placing glass or bamboo cups on the skin after creating a vacuum (by heat or suction) to draw up the skin and underlying tissues, promoting local blood and qi circulation.
把包放下	bǎ bāo fàng xià	put the bag down — 放 fàng (to place; put) + 下 xià (down; into a lower position). A simple directional 把 sentence , the object (bag) is moved to a lower location. 下 here signals downward movement or lowering into position. The 把 foregrounds the deliberate, targeted nature of the placing.
把窗户打开	bǎ chuāng hu dǎ kāi	to open the window (strike it into an open state) — 打 dǎ (to hit; to strike , used as a general action verb with many objects) + 开 kāi (open; apart; into an open state). The combination 打开 means "open" , the object is moved into an open or separated state. 开 as a resultative complement implies separation, opening, or dispersal.
把门关上	bǎ mén guān shàng	to close the door (shut it into the closed position) — 关 guān (to close) + 上 shàng (up; into a closed/attached state). Here 上 is a resultative complement indicating that the door has been moved into the closed, shut position.
把书放好	bǎ shū fàng hǎo	to put the book away properly — 放 fàng (to place; to put) + 好 hǎo (well; properly; in the right state). The 好 signals the object reaches a satisfactory, correct, or complete state. 放好 is not just "put down" , it's "place in a way that is done properly." 好 as a resultative complement implies care, correctness, and readiness.
把书拿出来	bǎ shū ná chū lái	take the book out (bring it out into the open) — 拿 ná (to take; to hold in the hand) + 出来 chūlái (out-coming; out toward the speaker). The book is extracted from some container or location and brought into the present space. 出来 implies emergence from a hidden or enclosed place toward an open, accessible position.
把握	bǎ wò	grip; certainty; to be confident
把椅子搬进来	bǎ yǐ zi bān jìn lái	bring the chair inside (move it in-coming direction) — 搬 bān (to move heavy things; to transport) + 进来 jìnlái (in-coming; into this space). The directional complement 进来 specifies both the direction (in) and the orientation toward the speaker (来). The 把 construction signals the chair is a definite, specific object being moved as a deliberate act.
把作业做完	bǎ zuò yè zuò wán	to finish the homework (do it through to completion) — 做 zuò (to do) + 完 wán (to finish; to exhaust; resultative complement). The 完 is the result , the homework has been completely done. Without 完, the sentence would be ungrammatical: *把作业做. 完 is one of the most common resultative complements in the 把 construction.
爸爸	bà ba	dad (colloquial)
霸道	bà dào	hegemony; domineering; the way of force — 霸 bà (hegemon; overlord, one who rules by force rather than virtue) + 道 dào (way; path). Classically: governing through coercion rather than moral example, the antithesis of 王道. Colloquially in modern Mandarin: overbearing, domineering (of a person's behavior).
霸主	bà zhǔ	hegemon lord — power exercised without moral mandate — 霸 bà (dominate by force) + 主 zhǔ (lord, master). Mencius drew the sharpest formulation of the distinction between 王 wáng (true kingship through moral virtue) and 霸 bà (hegemonic dominance through military and economic power): 以力假仁者霸 "one who uses force while pretending to benevolence is a hegemon" (…
白白	bái bái	in vain; for nothing
白菜	bái cài	Chinese cabbage; bok choy family — 白 bái (white) + 菜 cài (vegetable). The pale-stalked cabbage that anchors northern winter cooking. So cheap and plentiful that 白菜价 "cabbage price" means dirt cheap.
白茶	bái chá	white tea — minimally processed; wither and dry
白吃	bái chī	to eat for free; to freeload
白毫	bái háo	white down — the fine hairs on young buds
白话	bái huà	vernacular Chinese; plain speech
白话文	bái huà wén	vernacular written Chinese — 白 bái (plain; white; clear; spoken) + 话 huà (speech; talk) + 文 wén (written text). Writing that matches how people actually speak. 白话 literally = "plain speech," the ordinary spoken language as opposed to the elevated classical register.
白酒	bái jiǔ	baijiu — the spirit most often paired with a hotpot meal
白莲社	Bái lián Shè	White Lotus Society (Huiyuan's)
白米	bái mǐ	white rice; polished rice
白切鸡	bái qiē jī	White-Cut Chicken
白色	bái sè	white (the color); the color white
白天	bái tiān	daytime
白衣	bái yī	white-robed (form of Guanyin)
白衣观音	Bái yī Guān yīn	White-Robed Guanyin — the most common home altar image — 白衣 báiyī (white robe) + 观音. The seated figure in a white robe, often with a willow branch and a small vase, depicted on or near a lotus throne. This is the Guanyin of domestic altars across Taiwan, Hong Kong, Macau, and Southeast Asian Chinese diaspora communities.
百分之	bǎi fēn zhī	percent; out of a hundred parts — 百 (hundred) + 分 fēn (parts, divide) + 之 zhī (of, possessive particle). Literally: "out of a hundred parts." Chinese writes percentages as 百分之X , 百分之五十 (50%), 百分之百 (100%).
百花	bǎi huā	a hundred flowers; the 1956 campaign
百货	bǎi huò	all kinds of goods; department store — 百 (all kinds of) + 货 huò (goods, merchandise). 百货公司 (bǎihuò gōngsī, "all-goods company") is the standard Chinese term for a department store.
百家	bǎi jiā	a hundred schools; all schools of thought
百科	bǎi kē	encyclopedia; all branches of knowledge — 百 (all) + 科 kē (branch of study, department, subject). The term for encyclopedia: 百科全书 (bǎikē quánshū, "complete book of all branches of knowledge"). 百度百科 is the major Chinese-language wiki encyclopedia.
百年	bǎi nián	a hundred years; a full generation; a lifetime — 百 (hundred) + 年 nián (year). A century, but also a metonym for a full human span or a generational unit of history. 百年大计 (bǎinián dàjì, "a great plan for a hundred years") means a long-term fundamental strategy.
百年屈辱	Bǎi nián Qū rǔ	Century of Humiliation
百日	bǎi rì	The hundred-day mourning period following a death — The hundred-day mourning period following a death. During this time the bereaved family observes restrictions on celebration and festive activity. A commemoration ceremony at the end of the hundred days formally closes the mourning period.
百姓	bǎi xìng	the common people; ordinary citizens — 百 (hundred; all) + 姓 xìng (surname). The full range of surnames = everyone who is not nobility. The warm, slightly literary word for "the people." 老百姓 (lǎobǎixìng) adds 老 (old, familiar) for a more affectionate, colloquial tone. Political speeches say 人民; everyday speech says 老百姓.
败北	bài běi	to be defeated; to suffer a loss — 败 (bài, to be defeated, to lose) + 北 (běi, to flee , the original meaning). The classical military compound where 北 carries its original "flight in defeat" meaning. Used in formal and literary contexts for losing a battle, an election, or a competition.
败坏	bài huài	to corrupt, ruin
拜拜	bài bai	to worship; offering ritual (Taiwan)
拜神明	bài shén míng	to venerate the gods; to pay respect at a deity's altar — 拜 bài (to bow; to worship; to pay respects) + 神明. The standard verb for the act of ritual veneration , burning incense, bowing, presenting offerings. Common in Taiwan, Fujian, and diaspora communities. The word 拜 is secular enough (拜访 = to visit) that 拜神明 does not sound archaic or overly formal.
拜堂	bài táng	the traditional wedding ceremony bow — 拜 bài (to bow; to pay respects) + 堂 táng (hall; the family hall). The three bows of the traditional Chinese wedding: first bow to Heaven and Earth (一拜天地), second bow to the parents (二拜高堂), third bow to each other (夫妻对拜). Each bow ratifies the marriage before a different authority.
班	bān	shift — 两班倒 (liǎng bān dǎo) is two rotating shifts; 加班 (jiābān) overtime. Shifts and headcount tell you real capacity better than a capacity figure does.
般若波罗蜜	bō rě bō luó mì	prajñā-pāramitā; perfection of wisdom
斑马	bān mǎ	zebra
搬	bān	move (objects)
办	bàn	to handle; to do (a matter)
办法	bàn fǎ	a way; a means; a solution — 办 bàn (to handle; to manage) + 法 fǎ (way). The practical way to get a concrete thing done or to solve a problem at hand: 想办法 "to think of a way," 没办法 "there's nothing to be done."
办公室	bàng ōng shì	office
半	bàn	Half — Half. Follows the measure word when added to a whole quantity (一个半, one and a half) and precedes it when alone (半个小时, half an hour). Also in 半天 (bàntiān, "half a day"), the standard exaggeration for "ages."
伴手礼	bàn shǒu lǐ	A hand gift — A hand gift: a gift brought in hand when visiting someone, the material expression of having thought of the host. Turning up empty-handed (空手 kōngshǒu) to visit someone's home, especially a first visit, is considered poor form. The 伴手礼 need not be elaborate; the gesture matters more than the object.
绊脚石	bàn jiǎo shí	stumbling block; obstacle — 绊 bàn (to trip) + 脚 + 石 shí (stone). The foot-tripping stone. Both literal (a stone in the path) and figurative (an obstacle to progress).
帮	bāng	to help
帮忙	bāng / / máng	to help (lend-a-busy-hand) — 帮 (assist) + 忙 (busyness): to take on someone's busyness. The polite request is 帮个忙 or, upgraded, 帮我一个大忙 (do me a big favor). Direct objects go on bare 帮.
帮派	bāng pài	gang; faction
帮手	bāng shǒu	helper; assistant — 帮 bāng (to help) + 手 shǒu (person; hand). A helper , someone who lends a hand. Also 帮忙 bāng máng (to help out , the common spoken form) and 帮助 bāngzhù (to assist , more formal).
帮我翻译一下	bāng wǒ fān yì yí xià	please translate this for me — For your interpreter, or for the English-speaking salesperson when the engineer goes too fast. Using an interpreter for one hard passage and Chinese for the rest is common and costs no face.
帮我一下	bāng wǒ yí xià	give me a hand; help me out — A model of 一下 as a politeness softener. The bare 帮我 ("help me") is blunt; adding 一下 frames it as a small, quick favor and sounds far friendlier. Note that 一下 can follow the object here (帮我一下) because 帮 takes a person directly. The same softening works across requests: 看一下, 等一下, 问一下.
绑	bǎng	to tie; to bind
榜样	bǎng yàng	role model; good example to follow — 榜 bǎng (a posted list, a board) + 样 yàng (model). A person held up as a pattern for others: 树立榜样 "to set an example," 学习的榜样 "a model to learn from."
棒喝	bàng hè	the shout-and-blow — spontaneous awakening techniques — 棒 bàng (staff, blow) + 喝 hè (shout, roar). Two of the most distinctive Chan teaching methods: a sudden shout (associated especially with the master Linji 临济, whose shout 喝 became the name of the Rinzai school) and a strike with a staff (associated with Deshan 德山).
包	bāo	to wrap, bundle — To wrap, to enclose , the verb for making dumplings (包饺子), as well as the root of 包子 (stuffed bun). Also means "to guarantee" or "to take responsibility for."
包间	bāo jiān	private dining room
包装	bāo zhuāng	packaging — 彩盒 (cǎihé) is the retail colour box, 内盒 the inner box, 外箱 (wàixiāng) the master carton, 装箱数 the units per carton. 唛头 (màitóu), from English "mark," is the shipping mark printed on cartons. 跌落测试 (diēluò cèshì) is the drop test.
宝船	bǎo chuán	treasure ship (Zheng He's flagship)
饱餐	bǎo cān	to eat a hearty meal — 饱 (full) + 餐 (cān, meal): to eat heartily, to feast. Often appears as 饱餐一顿 (bǎocān yí dùn, to have a good filling meal), with a faint literary warmth.
饱含	bǎo hán	to be brimming with — 饱 (full) + 含 (hán, to hold inside): to be charged with, brimming with. Used for tears, feeling, or meaning, as in 饱含深情 (bǎohán shēnqíng, brimming with deep feeling).
饱和	bǎo hé	saturated, saturation — 饱 (full) + 和 (hé, to mix, to combine): saturation. A technical term for a solution, market, or air that can absorb no more, as in 市场已经饱和 (shìchǎng yǐjīng bǎohé, the market is already saturated).
饱满	bǎo mǎn	full, plump; brimming — 饱 (full) + 满 (mǎn, full to the brim): plump and rounded, or figuratively brimming with vigor. Describes grain, fruit, and spirits alike, as in 精神饱满 (jīngshén bǎomǎn, full of energy).
保持	bǎo chí	to keep, to maintain
保护	bǎo hù	to protect
保密协议	bǎo mì xié yì	confidentiality agreement — The general term for an NDA. NNN协议 is the China-specific form. Ask for it to be signed and sealed before you send drawings, not after.
保全	bǎo quán	to preserve intact; to keep safe and whole — 保 bǎo (to protect) + 全 quán (intact). To keep something whole and unharmed through a threat: 保全性命 "to preserve one's life," 保全名誉 "to keep one's reputation intact." The intact sense made active.
报仇	bào chóu	to avenge, take revenge — 报 (to requite) plus 仇 (chóu, enmity): to pay back a wrong. The dark twin of 报答, turning the same logic of return toward vengeance.
报酬	bào chou	remuneration, pay
报答	bào dá	to repay (a kindness) — 报 (to requite) plus 答 (dá, to answer): to answer a kindness with thanks made real. Reserved for repaying gratitude, a warmer word than the neutral 回报.
报道	bào dào	news report; to report (news) — 报 bào (to announce; to report; newspaper) + 道 dào (way; path). To map and announce the course of events: journalism as the act of charting what has happened. Used both as a noun (a report, a piece of coverage) and as a verb (to report on something).
报告	bào gào	to report; a report — 报 (to announce) plus 告 (gào, to tell): to deliver an account formally, and the document that delivers it. Works as both the act of reporting and the written or spoken report itself.
报价	bào jià	quotation
报价单	bào jià dān	quotation sheet — 报价 (bàojià) is both the verb "to quote" and the noun "quote." Ask for it in writing, line by line: 分项报价 (fēnxiàng bàojià) is an itemised quote that breaks out the product, packaging, tooling, and printing.
报警	bào jǐng	to call the police; sound an alarm
报名	bào míng	to sign up, register — 报 (to report) plus 名 (míng, name): to report your name onto a list. The standard verb for signing up or enrolling for a class, a contest, or an exam.
报社	bào shè	newspaper office; press — 报 bào (newspaper) + 社 shè (an agency, office). The organization that produces a newspaper, both the institution and its premises: 在报社工作 "to work at a newspaper." The 社 here is the institutional body, not a club; the same slot gives 出版社 "publishing house" and 杂志社 "magazine office."
报销	bào xiāo	to claim reimbursement
报纸	bào zhǐ	newspaper — 报 (the thing announced) plus 纸 (zhǐ, paper): the daily report rendered on paper. The everyday word for a printed newspaper, and the head noun behind titles like 《人民日报》.
暴力	bào lì	violence; brute force — 暴 bào (violent; cruel) + 力 lì (force). Force used to harm, the dark side of 力: 家庭暴力 "domestic violence," 暴力行为 "violent behavior." Here strength has turned destructive.
暴雨	bào yǔ	torrential rain; downpour
悲	bēi	sorrow; grief
悲观	bēi guān	pessimistic
悲悯	bēi mǐn	sympathy and compassion; compassionate pity — 悲 (grief at suffering) + 悯 mǐn (pity; compassion for the unfortunate). A literary compound for the quality of being moved by others' misfortune , closer to pity or sympathy than the more active 慈悲.
悲伤	bēi shāng	sad; grieved
北斗七星	běi dǒu qī xīng	the seven stars of the Big Dipper — 北斗 (the Northern Dipper) + 七星 (seven stars). The seven bright stars of the Big Dipper, central to Chinese astronomy, navigation, and Daoist ritual.
北方	běi fāng	the North; northern
北港朝天宫	Běi gǎng Cháo tiān Gōng	Beigang Chaotian Temple — Taiwan's great Mazu shrine — The Chaotian Temple (朝天宫) in Beigang, Yunlin County, Taiwan, is one of the oldest and most revered Mazu temples on the island, traditionally dating its founding to 1694 CE. With Dajia's Zhenlangong, it is one of Taiwan's two dominant Mazu pilgrimage centers.
北京	Běi jīng	Beijing (capital of China) — 北 (north) + 京 (capital). The Northern Capital , counterpart to 南京 Nánjīng (Southern Capital). Beijing has served as the capital of the Yuan, Ming, and Qing dynasties, and of the People's Republic. The name dates to 1403 when the Yongle Emperor moved the Ming court north from Nanjing.
北京话	Běi jīng huà	Beijing dialect — Beijing dialect: the basis of standard Mandarin (普通话 pǔtōnghuà). Beijing speech is characterized by heavy retroflex consonants and the 儿化 (érhuà) suffix that makes 这里 → 这儿, 哪里 → 哪儿. A strong Beijing accent is audible and distinct.
北洋军	Běi yáng Jūn	Beiyang Army (Yuan's power base)
背	bèi	back; to carry on the back
背后	bèi hòu	behind someone's back; in secret — 背 bèi (back of the body; to turn one's back on) + 后 (behind). The space behind a person's back , the zone they cannot see. In figurative use, 背后 means to act covertly, without someone's knowledge or consent. 在背后说人坏话 (to speak ill of someone behind their back) is a strong social criticism.
背诵	bèi sòng	to recite from memory
被打了	bèi dǎ le	got hit; was beaten (by an unspecified agent) — When the agent is unknown, irrelevant, or deliberately omitted, the 被 construction works without naming who did the action. The 被 alone signals passive and adversarial meaning.
被代表	bèi dài biǎo	to have one's views "represented" without consent — 代表 dàibiǎo (to represent; a representative). 被代表 = someone else speaks for you without your authorization and claims to represent your interests or views.
被动	bèi dòng	passive
被发现了	bèi fā xiàn le	was discovered; got caught (typically unwanted) — Discovery can be neutral or positive in English (a new species was discovered), but in Chinese the 被 frame typically implies the subject was caught or uncovered against its will or to its disadvantage. 被发现 often implies that someone was hiding something that has now been exposed.
被误解了	bèi wù jiě le	was misunderstood (adversarial — not one's intention) — Misunderstanding, misrepresentation, and mistreatment are quintessential 被 territory. The speaker's intention or the subject's nature is distorted by someone else's interpretation. The adversarial quality is strong , no one wants to be misunderstood.
被自杀	bèi zì shā	to be "suicided" — killed and declared a suicide — The most striking 被XX neologism. 自杀 zìshā means "to kill oneself" , a self-directed, voluntary act. Making it passive (被自杀) creates a grammatical impossibility that points to a political reality: a death classified as suicide that was, in the speaker's understanding, not self-chosen.
奔跑	bēn pǎo	to dash; to run hard; to gallop — 奔 (bēn, to rush; to gallop) + 跑. A more vivid and literary form than plain 跑. 奔 originally described the gallop of a horse; 奔跑 carries that sense of full-speed exertion.
本草	běn cǎo	Materia medica — Materia medica: the traditional term for the pharmacological literature and its subject matter.
本草纲目	běn cǎo gāng mù	the materia medica
本纪	běn jì	Basic Annals — rulers and dynasties — Twelve chapters chronicling the central line of power: the legendary sage-kings, the Xia, Shang, and Zhou, the Qin, and the Han emperors down to Wu.
本来	běn lái	originally; at first; (it goes without saying that) — 本 běn (root; origin) + 来 lái (come). What was the case from the start, before things changed: 我本来想去 "I originally meant to go (but didn't)." It often carries a quiet sense that the original state was the natural or proper one.
本领	běn lǐng	skill; capability
本人	běn rén	oneself; the person in question; I (formal) — 本 běn (this; one's own) + 人 rén (person). The very person concerned, as opposed to a representative or a rumor. On forms and in formal speech it means "I myself": 本人同意 "I personally agree."
本事	běn shi	ability; skill; what one is capable of — 本 běn (root; one's own) + 事 shì (matter). The capacities a person carries in themselves, their real skill rather than their luck or connections: 有本事 "to have what it takes." A close synonym is 本领 běnlǐng.
本土	běn tǔ	native; local; indigenous; domestic — 本 běn (root; origin; this) + 土 tǔ (soil; native place). Native, local, domestic, contrasted with 外来 wàilái (from outside) or 进口 jìnkǒu (imported). 本土品牌 = domestic brand. 本土文化 = local culture.
本性	běn xìng	innate nature; one's true nature — 本 běn (root, origin) + 性 xìng (nature). The nature one is born with, deep and hard to change: 本性善良 "good-natured at the core." Often invoked when surface behavior gives way to what a person really is, as in 本性难移, that one's nature is hard to alter.
本质	běn zhì	essence
本子	běn zi	notebook; exercise book; pad — 本 běn (volume) + 子 zi (suffix). A blank or ruled book to write in. Here 本 takes the toneless noun-suffix 子, exactly as in 桌子 and 房子.
笨蛋	bèn dàn	idiot, fool — 笨 (bèn, stupid, clumsy) + 蛋: a fool or dimwit. Among close friends it softens into teasing, even affection, but aimed at a stranger it lands as a real insult.
鼻	bí	nose
鼻子	bí zi	nose
比如	bǐ rú	for instance
比如说	bǐ rú shuō	"For example" or "like, for instance — "For example" or "like, for instance." Introduces a concrete illustration of an abstract point. Among the most useful phrases for explaining anything. The shorter 比如 works identically; 比如说 just adds a beat of preparation.
笔法	bǐ fǎ	brush method; stroke technique
笔画	bǐ huà	stroke; brushstroke — 笔 bǐ (brush; pen) + 画 huà (to draw; stroke). The individual strokes that make up a character. Chinese characters are analyzed by stroke count (几画 jǐ huà "how many strokes?") and stroke order (笔顺 bǐshùn).
笔记本	bǐ jì běn	notebook; laptop computer — 笔记 bǐjì + 本 běn (a bound volume; classifier for books). Originally: a bound notebook for handwritten jottings. Now also: laptop computer (笔记本电脑 bǐjìběn diànnǎo), the device that replaced the paper notebook for many people, while keeping the name.
笔名	bǐ míng	Pen name — Pen name: a literary pseudonym. Many Chinese writers and poets are better known by their pen names than their given names. Lu Xun (鲁迅) was born Zhou Shuren (周树人); his pen name is how he is universally known.
笔墨	bǐ mò	brush and ink; brushwork as a concept
笔顺	bǐ shùn	stroke order
笔者	bǐ zhě	the present writer; this author (formal self-reference) — 笔 (brush; pen; writing) + 者. Used in formal written Chinese as a modest self-reference: "the one holding the brush," meaning the author of the text you are reading.
必定	bì dìng	certainly; definitely; without a doubt — 必 bì (certainly) + 定 dìng (settled; fixed; determined). A speaker's confident prediction about the future or about something not yet observed. The certainty is in the speaker's judgment, not a rule or law. Closer to "I am certain that" than to "you are required to."
必然	bì rán	inevitable; necessarily; of necessity — 必 bì (certainly; without exception) + 然 rán (so; thus; naturally so , the word for "naturally" or "as it must be"). Together: that which is naturally and necessarily the case, which could not have been otherwise. The register is philosophical, historical, or scientific.
必胜	bì shèng	certain to win; victory is assured — 必 bì (certainly; without exception) + 胜 shèng (to win; to overcome). A morale-building formula rather than a measured prediction. Used in sports, military contexts, and competitive settings where the declaration of certainty is itself motivational. The certainty is willed, not merely predicted.
必须承认	bì xū chéng rèn	one must admit; it must be acknowledged that — A common rhetorical move in essays and serious conversation, conceding ground before pivoting. The 必须 here is not commanding the listener; it is the speaker acknowledging an inescapable point. English equivalent: "it has to be said that," "one must concede that."
必须遵守	bì xū zūn shǒu	must comply with; must abide by (rules, laws) — The standard pairing in legal, regulatory, and policy language. 必须遵守法律 (must comply with the law). The phrase carries the formal-rules register that 必须 lives in: every Chinese workplace handbook, public notice, and company memo will use this collocation rather than its softer alternatives.
必需	bì xū	essential (thing); necessity
必要	bì yào	necessary; required; essential (for a given purpose) — 必 bì (certainly; without exception) + 要 yào (to want; to need; required). Necessity defined by a purpose or goal: if you want the outcome, this is what the outcome requires. The conditionality is built in.
毕业	bì yè	to graduate; graduation — 毕 bì (to finish; to complete) + 业 yè (studies). To complete one's course of study: 大学毕业 "to graduate from university," 毕业典礼 "graduation ceremony." A separable verb, so the object slots in the middle: 毕了业 "have graduated."
闭	bì	to close, to shut (formal)
庇佑	bì yòu	divine protection and blessing
避雷针	bì léi zhēn	lightning rod — lit. "thunder-avoiding needle" — 避 bì (to avoid; to deflect) + 雷 léi (thunder/lightning) + 针 zhēn (needle; pin). Lightning rod. Literally: the needle that deflects the thunder. Metaphorically, a 避雷针 is a person or thing that absorbs blame or controversy on behalf of someone else , a lightning rod in the political sense.
璧	bì	jade disc
边关	biāng uān	border pass; frontier checkpoint — 边 (biān, border; edge) + 关 (guān, pass; strategic gate). The fortified checkpoints through the wall where military control, taxation, and passage were managed.
边角料	biān jiǎo liào	offcuts; scraps; odds and ends
边境	biān jìng	border; frontier
边远	biān yuǎn	remote and peripheral (border regions)
鞭炮	biān pào	firecrackers
变成	biàn chéng	to turn into; to become — 变 biàn (to change) + 成 chéng (to become; to complete; to succeed), the directional complement. The standard compound for completed transformation: X has fully become Y. 成 is the result complement, indicating the transformation has landed somewhere definite.
变法	biàn fǎ	to change the laws; political-legal reform (historical term) — 变 biàn (to change) + 法 fǎ (law; method; the Dharma, depending on context). The classical Chinese term for fundamental legal and institutional reform: changing the very rules by which a state operates.
变革	biàng é	transformation; systemic reform; institutional change — 变 biàn (to change) + 革 gé (to reform; leather, the image of tanning a hide and stripping away the old surface). 变革 carries more structural weight than 变化: it implies change to systems, institutions, or fundamental arrangements, not merely surface conditions.
变卦	biàng uà	changing hexagram; colloquial: to change one's mind, go back on one's word — In the 易经, 变卦 biànguà is the technical term for the resultant hexagram, the second hexagram produced when changing lines (爻 yáo that have reached their extreme) transform. The 变卦 reveals where the present situation is moving.
变化	biàn huà	change; transformation; variation — 变 biàn (to change) + 化 huà (to transform; to dissolve into something new). The most general and most frequent word for change in modern Mandarin. Where 变 alone is the verb of transformation, 变化 is the noun: change as a thing that happens, can be observed, measured, discussed.
变坏	biàn huài	to go bad, to deteriorate — 变 (to change) + 坏 (bad). To turn for the worse, whether food spoiling in the heat, weather souring, or a person's character degrading over time.
便	biàn	then; thereupon (literary)
便宜	pián yi	cheap, inexpensive
便宜一点	pián yi yì diǎn	a bit cheaper — The basic request, best softened. 价格有点高 (the price is a bit high) states the problem without accusing; 还有空间吗 (is there still room?) invites a counteroffer without naming one.
辨认	biàn rèn	to identify; distinguish
辩论	biàn lùn	to debate; to argue (a formal case) — 辩 biàn (to dispute, to argue points) + 论. To debate opposing positions, each side marshalling arguments to win. The standard word for a debate competition (辩论赛 biànlùnsài) and for adversarial argument generally.
辫子	biàn zi	the Manchu queue hairstyle
标点	biāo diǎn	punctuation
标记	biāo jì	a marker; a label; to mark — 标 biāo (to label, to mark out) + 记 jì (a mark). A marker or tag that identifies a thing, and the act of placing it: 在地图上标记位置 "to mark a location on a map."
标题	biāo tí	headline; title; caption — 标 biāo (to mark; label) + 题 tí (heading). The title line printed at the top of an article, book, or section: 新闻标题 "a news headline," 大标题 "a big banner headline." Literally the marked heading.
表达	biǎo dá	to express: to convey; to articulate (thoughts and feelings) — 表 biǎo (to bring to the surface) + 达 dá (to reach; to arrive at; to get through). To convey thoughts or feelings so that they reach another person clearly. The 达 element adds the sense of successful transmission: not just showing something, but getting it across.
表哥	biǎo gē	older male cousin — 表 (maternal/external) + 哥. An older male cousin on the mother's side, or through a paternal aunt. The 表 prefix marks cousins outside the father's patriline, distinct from 堂哥 (tánggē).
表格	biǎo gé	form: a table; a chart; a grid laid out for filling in — 表 biǎo (a chart; a surface where data is laid out) + 格 gé (a grid; a square; a cell). A printed or digital form, table, or grid: anything with rows and cells to be read or filled in.
表姐	biǎo jiě	older female cousin
表面	biǎo miàn	surface; outside; outward appearance — 表 biǎo (outer; to show) + 面 miàn (face). The outer surface of a thing, and by extension the surface meaning as opposed to the truth beneath: 表面上 biǎomiàn shàng "on the surface, ostensibly."
表面处理	biǎo miàn chǔ lǐ	surface finish — The finishing family: 抛光 (pāoguāng) polishing, 电解 (diànjiě) electrolytic polishing for liners, 喷涂 (pēntú) spray painting, 喷粉 (pēnfěn) powder coating, 丝印 (sīyìn) screen printing, 镭雕 (léidiāo) laser engraving. 涂层 (túcéng) is the coating itself; 掉漆 (diàoqī) is paint coming off.
表皮	biǎo pí	outer skin: the epidermis; the outermost layer or rind — 表 biǎo (outer) + 皮 pí (skin; hide; rind). The outermost layer of a living thing: the epidermis of human skin, the rind of a fruit, the bark-like surface of a plant. A precise term used in biology, medicine, and everyday talk about peels and surfaces.
表情	biǎo qíng	facial expression
表示	biǎo shì	to express: to indicate; to show (a feeling, attitude, or position) — 表 biǎo (to bring to the surface) + 示 shì (to show; to indicate; to make manifest). To express or indicate an attitude, opinion, or feeling, often through a word, gesture, or symbolic act. 表示 frequently introduces what someone states or signals: 表示同意 (express agreement), 表示感谢 (express thanks).
表现	biǎo xiàn	to show; to display; (noun) performance, conduct, showing — 表 biǎo (outer; to express) + 现 xiàn (show). To make an inner quality visible on the outside. As a noun it is one's showing or conduct: 他今天表现很好 "he did very well today."
表演	biǎo yǎn	to perform: to act; a performance; a show — 表 biǎo (to show outwardly) + 演 yǎn (to act; to perform; to develop). To perform before an audience, and the performance itself: music, dance, drama, magic, acrobatics.
别	bié	don't (imperative negation)
别的	bié de	other (colloquial)
别客气	bié kè qi	don't stand on ceremony
别人	bié rén	other people; someone else (colloquial) — 别 bié (other; different; another) + 人 rén. The default, everyday word for "other people" in spoken Mandarin. Neutral register, very high frequency. Used in advice, comparisons, complaints, and social commentary; the most natural choice whenever you mean "other people" in conversation.
宾馆	bīng uǎn	hotel, guesthouse — 宾 bīn (guest) + 馆. A house for guests, that is, a hotel. The word keeps the old hospitality sense of 馆 right on its sleeve.
濒危	bīn wēi	endangered (species)
冰	bīng	ice
兵法	bīng fǎ	military strategy; the art of war
并非	bìng fēi	is not in fact; really is not — 并 bìng (actually; emphatic) + 非 fēi (not). A firm, slightly formal "is not at all," used to push back against an assumption: 并非如此 "that is not the case," 并非不可能 "not in fact impossible."
并且	bìng qiě	and; furthermore; as well as — 并 bìng (side by side) + 且 qiě (further). Links two actions or qualities on an even footing, often joining verbs: 讨论并且决定 "to discuss and decide." Neutral where 而且 leans emphatic.
病毒	bìng dú	virus — 病 + 毒 (dú, poison): a "sickness poison," the standard word for a biological virus and, by extension, a computer virus. The same term covers the flu and the malware on your hard drive.
病房	bìng fáng	hospital ward, sickroom — 病 + 房 (fáng, room): the room where patients are kept, a hospital ward. It pairs naturally with 病床 (bìngchuáng, hospital bed), bringing the original sickbed of the radical 疒 full circle.
病故	bìng gù	to die of illness; to pass away from sickness — 病 (bìng, illness) + 故 (here, to pass away). A measured, somewhat formal way to say that someone died of illness, used in obituaries, official notices, and dignified speech. The 故 draws on the deceased sense: the dead belong now to what has passed.
病假	bìng jià	sick leave
病菌	bìng jūn	germ, pathogenic bacterium — 病 + 菌 (jūn, bacterium, fungus): a disease-causing germ. Where 病毒 names a virus, 病菌 names the bacterial culprit, the everyday word a parent uses when telling a child to wash their hands.
病情	bìng qíng	condition of an illness — 病 + 情 (qíng, situation, state): the state of a sickness, how the illness stands. Doctors use it to report whether a patient is stable, worsening, or improving.
病人	bìng rén	patient, sick person — 病 + 人 (rén, person): a sick person, the general word for a patient in a hospital or clinic. It names the person by their condition rather than their role, unlike 患者 (huànzhě), which is the more clinical register used in medical writing.
玻璃被打破了	bō lí bèi dǎ pò le	the glass was broken — 打 dǎ (to hit) + 破 pò (broken; shattered , resultative complement). The 破 is the result that makes the sentence grammatical. Breakage is typically undesirable , a window, a vase, a screen. The 被 construction here makes the glass the focus: it is the thing that suffered the breaking.
伯牙	Bó Yá	the master qin player (Spring and Autumn period)
博物馆	bó wù guǎn	museum — 博 bó (broad, extensive) + 物 wù (things) + 馆. The hall of broad things, where relics and specimens are kept and shown. 故宫博物院 uses 院 instead, but the everyday word is 博物馆.
补	bǔ	to mend; to patch; to supplement
补救	bǔ jiù	to remedy; to make good; remediation
补语	bǔ yǔ	complement
不必	bù bì	not necessary; need not
不错	bú cuò	pretty good, not bad; correct, right — 不 bù (not) + 错 cuò (wrong). Two readings from one shape: as praise, "quite good, not bad" (成绩不错 "good grades"); as confirmation, "right, correct" (一点不错 "exactly right"). Note the tone sandhi: 不 shifts to bú before the falling 错.
不但	bú dàn	not only
不道德	bù dào dé	immoral; unethical; morally wrong — 不 bù (negation) + 道德. The direct negation , applied to actions, behavior, people, and policies alike. Notably versatile in everyday condemnation: this is the word a person reaches for in an argument when something feels wrong without being illegal.
不得不	bù dé bù	cannot but; have no choice but to — 不得不 (not-obtain-not) = double negation creates strong obligation: "cannot not do" = must do, compelled by circumstances. Stronger than 必须 bìxū in the sense of reluctant necessity. The speaker has no choice.
不得了	bù dé liǎo	extremely; terrible; amazing (intensifier) — 不 + 得了 déliǎo (can be concluded; can be over). "Cannot be concluded" → extreme beyond measure. Used after adjectives as a strong intensifier: 好得不得了 = incredibly good. Also standalone: 不得了！= This is unbelievable / It's a disaster! (depending on context).
不懂	bù dǒng	to not understand
不都	bù dōu	not all; not every one (partial negation) — 不 precedes 都. The negation falls on the universal claim: "it's not the case that all of them do X." Some do, some don't. This is logically weaker than 都不 and much more common in careful, qualified speech.
不断	bú duàn	continuously; without stopping
不妨	bù fáng	might as well; no harm in
不过	bù guò	"However" or "but" (softer) — "However" or "but" (softer). The default conversational pivot. Lighter than 但是, which can feel direct or even adversarial in spoken contexts. 不过 carries a gentler "that said…" tone, ideal for adding a qualification.
不好意思	bù hǎo yì si	embarrassed; sorry to trouble you; excuse me — Lit: not-good-meaning/face. The phrase used when imposing on someone, asking a favor, or causing mild embarrassment. One of the most important social lubricants in Mandarin. Not a strong apology, more like "I feel awkward about this" or "pardon the imposition."
不会吧	bù huì ba	"No way!" or "you can't be serious — "No way!" or "you can't be serious." Expresses disbelief, often at bad news or an unwelcome outcome. The 吧 softens it from an outright denial into an incredulous reaction.
不见了	bú jiàn le	to be missing, to have disappeared
不仅	bù jǐn	not merely (formal pair for 而且)
不可	bù kě	must not; absolutely cannot; impermissible — The strong negation of 可: not merely "cannot" (unable) but "cannot be permitted to." Formal and classical in flavor. 不可 carries the weight of prohibition rather than incapability.
不可能	bù kěn éng	impossible; cannot be — The flat negation, "impossible, no way." 这不可能 ("this is impossible"), 不可能的事 ("an impossible thing"). It is strong and absolute, unlike the softer 不太可能 ("unlikely") which leaves a sliver of chance. As an exclamation, 不可能！means "no way! / that can't be!"
不客气	bú kè qi	"you're welcome"; impolite, blunt
不快	bù kuài	unhappy; displeased; feeling off — 不 bù (negation) + 快 kuài. The heart is not flowing freely , something is blocking it. Milder and more internal than 不高兴 (visibly in a bad mood); 不快 often describes a quiet dissatisfaction or sense of offense that hasn't been expressed.
不来了	bù lái le	not coming anymore; decided not to come — 不…了 is the negation of sentence-final 了 , it signals a reversal of an expected or prior state. The 了 here paradoxically signals a new situation: the new situation is "not doing X anymore." Extremely common for announcing changed plans, habits that have been given up, or states that have ended.
不劳而获者	bù láo ér huò zhě	one who gains without working; one who reaps without effort — 不劳而获 (to gain without labor) + 者. A full predicate (verb phrase plus adverb) nominalized by 者. The resulting noun phrase can serve as subject, object, or object of a preposition. This is 者 at its most generative: even a four-character predicate can be converted into a noun phrase by appending 者.
不良率	bù liáng lǜ	defect rate — Percentage of defective units; 良品率 or 良率 (liánglǜ) is the yield, its complement. 不良品 (bùliángpǐn) and 次品 (cìpǐn) are defective goods. Agree an acceptable rate in the contract and what happens above it.
不论	bù lùn	no matter; regardless of — 不 bù (not) + 论. Functionally interchangeable with 无论 in most sentences, slightly less formal. Same grammar: an open element follows, and 都 or 也 marks the main clause.
不明不白	bù míng bù bái	unclear, fishy, ambiguous
不平等条约	bù píng děng tiáo yu ē	unequal treaties
不然	bù rán	otherwise; or else; if not so — 不 bù (not) + 然 rán (so). Literally "if it is not so," hence "otherwise." Warns of the alternative outcome: do this, 不然 the bad thing happens. Close to 否则 fǒuzé but more colloquial.
不仁	bù rén	inhumane; callous; morally numb — also: physical numbness (limbs without sensation) — 不 bù (not; without) + 仁 rén (humaneness). The negation that reveals the concept's full weight: to be without 仁 is not merely to be unkind , it is to be morally deadened, incapable of feeling for others.
不认真	bú rèn zhēn	not taking it seriously; phoning it in
不如	bù rú	not as good as; would be better to — 不 bù (not) + 如 rú (as good as). Either ranks one thing below another (A 不如 B, "A is not as good as B") or proposes a better course ("we'd be better off..."). Context tells the two apart.
不食人间烟火	bù shí rén jiān yān huǒ	"not eating the smoke and fire of the human world" — otherworldly; too aloof to be of this world — Literally: "does not eat the smoke and fire of 人间." Originally said of immortals (仙 xiān) and deities, who subsist on pure air rather than cooked food and are therefore free of the desires and needs that tie mortals to this world.
不是	bú shì	is not; to not be; it's not that… — The negation of 是 , always 不是, never 没是 (unlike verbs of action, which can be negated with 没 for past tense).
不是我说的	bú shì wǒ shuō de	it wasn't me who said it (correcting the agent) — The classic defensive use of 不是…的: denying agency for a known past statement or action. Both parties know something was said , the 不是…的 corrects the attribution. This is one of the most practically useful forms of the construction in daily social life.
不是昨天买的	bú shì zuó tiān mǎi de	it wasn't bought yesterday (correcting the time) — Time correction: both speakers know the item was bought , the correction is about when. 不是昨天 denies the specific time; the speaker may then supply the correct time with 是…的.
不舒服	bù shū fu	Unwell — Unwell: "not comfortable." The all-purpose symptom, the polite sick-day excuse, and the doctor's opening question in negative form: 哪里不舒服?
不太	bú tài	not very; not too; not quite — 不 bù (not) + 太 tài (very). The gentle negative, softer than a bare 不: 不太喜欢 "don't really like it," 不太方便 "not very convenient." The standard polite way to decline or downplay.
不太会喝酒	bú tài huì hē jiǔ	not much of a drinker — Decide before the dinner whether you drink, and say it early with a reason that leaves no one at fault: health, medication, an early meeting, or driving. Once you have drunk one toast, refusing the next is harder.
不谈生意	bù tán shēng yi	no business talk — Price is rarely negotiated at the table, but the boss may ask a soft question about volume or the future. Answer warmly and without numbers; numbers belong in the meeting room the next day.
不同	bù tóng	different; not the same; difference — 不 bù (not) + 同 tóng (same). The standard word for "different": 不同的意见 "differing opinions." As a noun it names the difference itself: 两者的不同 "the difference between the two."
不退转	bù tuì zhuǎn	non-regression
不喜欢	bù xǐ huan	to not like; to dislike — Negate with 不 before the verb. To soften, insert 太: 不太喜欢 ("don't really like") is gentler and more polite than a flat 不喜欢. For a stronger feeling, 讨厌 tǎoyàn means "to find annoying, to dislike intensely."
不孝	bù xiào	unfilial
不行	bù xíng	not OK; won't do; no good; (terminally) declining — 不 bù + 行 xíng. The everyday refusal: "no, that won't work." 行 on its own as an interjection means "OK, fine, that'll do" , and 不行 is its negation. Also extends to "not capable" (我数学不行 "I'm bad at math") and, in context of illness or business, "in serious decline" (他不行了 "he's failing / fading").
不锈钢	bù xiù gāng	stainless steel — Grades by number: 201 is cheaper and less corrosion-resistant, 304 is the common food-contact grade, 316 adds molybdenum for better resistance to acids and salt. 材质 (cáizhì) is "material" in the sense of grade and composition.
不要	bú yào	don't (prohibition)
不一定	bù yī dìng	"Not necessarily — "Not necessarily." A gentle pushback that leaves room for the other person's view to be partly right. Often used to introduce a qualification: 不一定吧, 也可能是… ("not necessarily, it could also be…").
不一样	bù yí yàng	different
不应该	bù yīng gāi	should not; ought not to — Negates the obligation: putting 不 before 应该 says something ought not to be done: 你不应该对她说谎 ("you shouldn't lie to her"). Often it voices regret about the past, "should not have": 我不应该那样说 ("I shouldn't have said that"). Do not confuse with 应该不, where 不 falls after and negates a guessed event instead.
不用	bù yòng	no need to; don't have to
不用客气	bú yòng kè qi	no need to be polite
不知道	bù zhī dào	don't know; I'm not sure; I have no idea — 不 bù (negation) + 知道. One of the most common phrases in conversational Mandarin: the honest, unadorned acknowledgment of not having found the path. As a standalone response, 不知道！is perfectly natural and socially acceptable (not rude) in most contexts.
不足	bù zú	insufficient; not enough; shortcoming — 不 (not) + 足 (sufficient). The negation of sufficiency. 时间不足 (insufficient time); 不足为奇 (not enough to be surprising , meaning "nothing to be surprised about"); 美中不足 (a flaw in an otherwise perfect situation , literally "not enough in the beautiful center").
布局	bù jú	Composition — Composition: the arrangement of characters and space within the seal face. Considered the most demanding aspect of seal design: the carver must balance the visual weight of characters with varying graphic complexity, distribute space evenly, and create a design that reads as a coherent unit when imp…
布施	bù shī	generosity; almsgiving
步行街	bù xíng jiē	pedestrian street
部队	bù duì	military unit; troops; armed forces — 部 bù (unit) + 队 duì (team; formation). A body of troops under one command, from a single unit up to the armed forces in general: 参军入部队 "to join the army." Soldiers say 在部队 "in the service."
部分	bù fen	part; section; portion — 部 bù (section) + 分 fen (a share). A part of something larger, the everyday word for a portion: 大部分 dàbùfen "the greater part, most of," 一部分 yí bùfen "a portion." Note the second syllable goes neutral.
部件	bù jiàn	component; assembly
部落	bù luò	tribe; clan
部门	bù mén	department; division; branch — 部 bù (section) + 门 mén (gate; category). One demarcated wing of a company or institution: 销售部门 "the sales department," 政府部门 "government departments." The standard word for an organizational unit.
部首	bù shǒu	radical (the section head of a character) — 部 bù (section, division) + 首 shǒu (head). The radical of a character: the head-element under which a dictionary groups it. 首 itself is a 部首. To look a character up in a paper dictionary, you find its 部首 first, then count the remaining strokes.
部长	bù zhǎng	minister; head of a department — 部 bù (ministry) + 长 zhǎng (chief; head). The head of a ministry or department: 外交部长 "the Foreign Minister." The 长 here is the leader-sense, read zhǎng, not the cháng of "long."
才华	cái huá	brilliance; flair
才两个人	cái liǎng gè rén	only two people (not enough — disappointing) — 才 before a quantity expresses that the amount is smaller than expected or desired. This is the scarcity / insufficiency use of 才: the number is barely enough, disappointing, a minimum.
才能	cái néng	ability; talent; capability — 才 cái (talent) + 能 néng (capacity). The abilities a person actually possesses and can put to use: 发挥才能 "to give full play to one's talents." Note the toned reading cáinéng (the noun); the adverb 才 plus 能 (cái néng, "only then can") is a separate construction.
才智	cái zhì	talent and wisdom; intellectual gifts; ability and discernment — 才 cái (talent; natural ability , the character shows a seedling breaking through the earth, raw capacity before cultivation) + 智 zhì (wisdom; discernment). The pairing of natural gift with cultivated understanding. 才智过人 cáizhì guòrén = talent and wisdom surpassing others.
材	cái	material; timber; talent
财富	cái fù	wealth; riches — 财 (money, property) plus 富 (riches). The accumulated stock of wealth itself, the noun for what a rich person holds. Often paired abstractly, as in 精神财富 (spiritual wealth).
财神	Cái shén	the Wealth God — most actively invoked deity in commercial life — Cáishén is not one deity but a cluster of wealth-associated gods, the most popular being 赵公明 Zhào Gōngmíng (a dark-faced general riding a black tiger) and the Five Roads Gods (五路财神 Wǔlù Cáishén). His image greets visitors at the entrances of shops, restaurants, and banks across China.
彩礼	cǎi lǐ	Betrothal gifts , the payment from the groom's family to the bride's family as part of the formal engagement. In contemporary China, the cash component (also called 彩礼钱) has become a source of social tension in areas where amounts have escalated sharply.
菜单	cài dān	menu; (computing) a menu — 菜 cài (dish) + 单 dān (list; slip). The list of dishes a restaurant offers, and by extension the drop-down 菜单 of a computer program. 看菜单 "to look at the menu."
菜鸟	cài niǎo	(slang) novice; newbie; noob — 菜 cài (here: weak, lousy) + 鸟 niǎo (bird). A fledgling, hence a beginner who is still bad at something, especially in gaming and the workplace. Self-deprecating or teasing: 我是个菜鸟 "I'm a total newbie."
菜系	cài xì	cuisine school; culinary lineage — Cuisine system , the term for a recognized regional cooking tradition. The 八大菜系 (eight great cuisine systems) are the canonical eight, though culinary scholars recognize anywhere from four to dozens of regional traditions.
参观	cāng uān	to visit; to tour (a place) — 参 cān (to take part) + 观 guān (to view). To visit a place in order to see it: a museum, factory, school, or exhibition. Reserved for touring sites, not for visiting people, where 看望 kànwàng or 拜访 bàifǎng is used instead.
餐厅	cān tīng	restaurant; dining hall
蚕丝	cán sī	Silk thread , literally "silkworm thread — Silk thread , literally "silkworm thread." The raw fiber before weaving. 蚕 is the silkworm; 丝 is the thread.
仓库	cāng kù	warehouse — 原材料仓 raw-material store, 成品仓 finished-goods store. A tidy warehouse with labelled batches (标识 biāoshí) is a good sign of traceability.
苍白	cāng bái	pallid, ashen, sickly white
操守	cāo shǒu	moral integrity
曹操	cáo cāo	Cao Cao — the archetype of cunning ambition — The warlord of the north, in the novel the great villain: a brilliant general, administrator, and poet, but ruthless and treacherous, the man who declares he would rather betray the world than let the world betray him.
曹洞	Cáo dòng	Caodong school (Soto in Japan)
草地	cǎo dì	lawn; grassland; a grassy patch — 草 cǎo (grass) + 地 dì (ground). Any stretch of ground covered in grass, from a park lawn to an open meadow. 在草地上 "on the grass."
草稿	cǎo gǎo	rough draft; manuscript draft — 草 cǎo (rough) + 稿 gǎo (manuscript). The first, unpolished version of a piece of writing or a plan. 打草稿 "to make a draft," to sketch something out before committing.
草率	cǎo shuài	careless; hasty; rash — 草 cǎo (rough; hasty) + 率 shuài (rate; rashly). Done quickly and without care, like grass thrown up overnight. 做事草率 "to do things carelessly."
草帽	cǎo mào	straw hat — 草 cǎo (straw) + 帽 mào (hat). A hat woven from dried straw, the broad-brimmed shade of farmers and summer beaches. 戴草帽 "to wear a straw hat."
草书	cǎo shū	cursive script — the most expressive and wild form — 草 cǎo (grass; rough; draft) + 书 shū. "Grass writing" , like wild grass, cursive is fast, flowing, and irregular. Individual strokes merge; some characters are radically abbreviated. Legible mainly to trained readers. The style most associated with artistic expression and emotional intensity.
草药	cǎo yào	herbal medicine; medicinal herbs — 草 cǎo (herb) + 药 yào (medicine). Medicine made from plants, the leaves and roots brewed in traditional Chinese pharmacy. Often expanded to 中草药 zhōng cǎoyào, Chinese herbal medicine.
草原	cǎo yuán	grassland; steppe; prairie — 草 cǎo (grass) + 原 yuán (plain). A vast open plain of grass, the wind-rippled steppe of Inner Mongolia or the prairies of the world. 内蒙古大草原 "the great grasslands of Inner Mongolia."
恻隐之心	cè yǐn zhī xīn	the heart of compassion / commiseration — sprout of 仁 humaneness — 恻隐 cèyǐn: the feeling of alarm and pity , specifically the involuntary distress one feels at another's suffering. The child-at-the-well example demonstrates this sprout. It is the first beginning of 仁 rén (humaneness/benevolence), the master virtue.
测量	cè liáng	to measure: to survey, to gauge with an instrument — 测 cè (to gauge, to fathom) + 量 liáng (to measure). The formal verb for taking precise measurements: surveying land, gauging distance, recording temperature or pressure with an instrument. 测量土地 (to survey land), 测量距离 (to measure distance).
曾国藩	Zēng Guó fān	Zeng Guofan, scholar-general
曾经	céng jīng	once, formerly
叉烧	chā shāo	Char Siu — Cantonese BBQ Pork
插一脚	chā yī jiǎo	to butt in; to insert oneself into someone else's affair — 插 chā (to insert) + 一 yī (one) + 脚. To stick a foot in where it wasn't invited , usually meddlesome but sometimes opportunistic. Cousin of English "to put one's foot in." Often pejorative.
插茱萸	chā zhū yú	wearing zhuyu sprigs — apotropaic plant, festival shorthand — 插 chā (to insert; to wear tucked in) + 茱萸 zhūyú (Cornus officinalis; Japanese cornelian cherry). The zhuyu shrub, with its fragrant berries and pungent scent, was believed to repel evil and disease. On Double Ninth, people wore sprigs tucked into their hair or carried them in small pouches.
茶餐厅	chá cān tīng	Cha chaan teng — Cha chaan teng: the quintessential Hong Kong casual restaurant, serving a fusion menu of Cantonese and Western-influenced dishes: milk tea, pineapple buns (菠萝包 bōluóbāo), egg tarts, congee, rice dishes, and Hong Kong-style French toast.
茶禅一味	chá chán yī wèi	tea and Chan: one flavor
茶馆	chá guǎn	Teahouse — Teahouse: a social institution especially important in Sichuan and Guangdong, where one goes not just to drink tea but to play mahjong, listen to storytelling, or simply spend a morning reading the paper.
茶毫	chá háo	tea down — fine white hairs on young leaves
茶荷	chá hé	tea display dish — for showing the dry leaf
茶壶	chá hú	Teapot — Teapot. The Yixing purple clay teapot (紫砂壶) is the prestige form, prized because it absorbs tea oils over time and improves with use.
茶黄素	chá huáng sù	theaflavins — the brightness compounds of black tea
茶夹	chá jiā	tea tongs — for handling hot cups
茶马古道	Chá Mǎ Gǔ Dào	Ancient Tea-Horse Road — the trade route that carried tea to Tibet
茶盘	chá pán	tea tray — the base of the gongfu setup
茶香	chá xiāng	aroma of tea
茶叶	chá yè	Tea leaves , the dry product before brewing — Tea leaves , the dry product before brewing. 茶 alone can refer to the beverage, the plant, or the leaves.
茶叶蛋	chá yè dàn	tea egg
茶叶罐	chá yè guàn	tea canister / storage jar
茶艺	chá yì	tea art; the art and ceremony of tea — 茶 chá + 艺 yì (art; skill, the same character as in 艺术 fine art). The performance and aesthetic dimension of tea preparation. 茶艺师 cháyìshī = tea master; a professional practitioner of the ceremony. 茶艺表演 = a tea ceremony performance.
茶针	chá zhēn	tea needle / pu-erh pick
差不多	chà bu duō	almost; about the same
差错	chā cuò	a slip, an error; a mishap going wrong — 差 chā (off, deviating) + 错 cuò (error). A small slip or accidental error, and by extension an unforeseen mishap: 万一出了差错 "in case something goes wrong." Both halves mean "off the mark," doubling the sense of deviation.
拆开	chāi kāi	to tear open; to unwrap
禅让	shàn ràng	to abdicate in favor of the worthy — The shàn reading's flagship: throne-yielding on the Yao-and-Shun model, the Confucian ideal of succession by virtue rather than blood, and the polite fiction wrapping most actual dynastic seizures.
禅宗	Chán Zōng	Chan Buddhism — meditation, direct transmission, sudden enlightenment — The most distinctively Chinese Buddhist school. Chan (from Sanskrit dhyāna, meditation) rejected scripture-based study in favor of direct mind-to-mind transmission between master and student, the use of paradoxical dialogues (公案 gōng'àn, kōans), and the possibility of sudden enlightenment 顿悟 dùnwù i…
产能	chǎn néng	production capacity — Output per day or month. 排产 (páichǎn) is scheduling an order onto the line; 旺季 (wàngjì) is peak season, when capacity disappears. In the months before Chinese New Year lines fill up and many workers leave early, so lead times stretch.
产品认证	chǎn pǐn rèn zhèng	product certification — 认证 rènzhèng (to certify, certification , 认 recognize + 证 proof/certificate). The system by which products are officially verified to meet standards. 3C认证 (China Compulsory Certification) is the mandatory certification for electronics, appliances, and vehicles sold in China.
产品质量	chǎn pǐn zhì liàng	product quality — 质量 zhìliàng (substance + quantity → quality) is the standard word for quality in both the everyday and technical sense. 产品质量 is the fixed phrase for product quality , appearing in consumer protection law (产品质量法), manufacturing standards, and marketing.
产前样	chǎn qián yàng	pre-production sample (PPS) — Made on the production line with production materials, after tooling and before bulk starts. Hand samples can be perfect while line samples are not; insisting on a 产前样 catches the difference before thousands of units exist.
产生	chǎn shēng	to produce; to give rise to; to emerge — 产 chǎn (to produce; output) + 生 shēng (arise). For abstract and concrete creation , ideas, problems, emotions, consequences arising. 产生影响 "to have an effect" · 产生误会 "to give rise to a misunderstanding."
常常	cháng cháng	often; frequently; time and again — The reduplicated 常. A common, conversational adverb for frequent action. Its negative is usually 不常 bùcháng "not often," since 不常常 is avoided.
常识	cháng shí	common sense; general knowledge — 常 cháng (constant; common) + 识 shí (knowledge). The basic knowledge that holds across a community, what one is expected to know: 生活常识 "everyday know-how," 缺乏常识 "to lack common sense."
嫦娥	Cháng é	Chang'e — the Moon Goddess
厂房	chǎng fáng	factory building
厂家	chǎng jiā	manufacturer
场	chǎng	open ground, venue
场所	chǎng suǒ	place; venue; site
唱反调	chàng fǎn diào	to deliberately disagree; to sing a different tune — 唱 chàng (to sing) + 反调 fǎndiào (an opposing tune). To voice the contrary position on purpose, to be the one who always sings against the prevailing note. Colloquial and faintly critical.
唱歌	chàng gē	to sing — 唱 (sing) + 歌 (song). The social institution attached is KTV (卡拉OK), where 唱歌 is what colleagues, classmates, and business contacts do together; declining every song is borderline antisocial, and one rehearsed number is sound survival strategy.
唱片	chàng piàn	record; disc (vinyl, album) — 唱 chàng (to sing) + 片 piàn (a thin flat piece). A singing-disc: the physical record that holds recorded sound. 唱片公司 chàngpiàn gōngsī is a record label. The word predates and survives digital formats.
唱腔	chàng qiāng	Vocal style , the melodic and tonal patterns of a particular role type. Different roles use different vocal registers and melodic conventions.
钞票	chāo piào	banknote: paper money; cash — 钞 chāo (paper money) + 票 piào (slip; note). A banknote, paper currency, cash in hand. This compound shows 票 at its purest: a printed slip of paper that stands for money. Closely related is 支票 zhīpiào (a cheque), where 支 zhī (to pay out) marks the slip as an instrument of payment.
超度	chāo dù	to deliver souls (Buddhist ritual)
超越	chāo yu è	to surpass; to exceed; to transcend — 超 (chāo, to overtake; to exceed) + 越. The figurative summit of the family: surpassing a competitor, exceeding a limit, transcending a category. Used in business, sport, philosophy, and self-development contexts.
朝代	cháo dài	dynasty: a reigning house and its era — 朝 cháo (court, reign) + 代 dài (generation, age). The standard word for a dynasty, the span during which one ruling house held the throne. Chinese history is told as a sequence of 朝代, each with its own name, capital, and character.
朝气	zhāo qì	youthful spirit: vigor, the lively air of a new day — 朝 zhāo (morning) + 气 qì (air, energy). The energy of morning seen in people, a fresh, lively spirit. Said of the young or of any group full of drive: 充满朝气 "brimming with vigor." Its opposite is 暮气 mùqì, the listlessness of dusk.
朝前	cháo qián	forward; toward the front; facing ahead — 朝 cháo (toward) + 前 qián (front). Directed at the space ahead: 朝前走 "walk forward," 朝前看 "look ahead." A close everyday counterpart to 向前.
朝圣	cháo shèng	pilgrimage
朝廷	cháo tíng	the imperial court: the ruler and the central government — 朝 cháo (court) + 廷 tíng (court hall). The ruler together with the ministers and offices around the throne, the seat of central power. To act 代表朝廷 is to act in the name of the throne itself.
朝夕	zhāo xī	morning and evening: every hour, day after day — 朝 zhāo (morning) + 夕 xī (evening). The two ends of the day standing for all of it: from dawn to dusk, every hour. 朝夕相处 "to be together morning and evening," to live in close daily company; 只争朝夕 "to seize every moment."
朝向	cháo xiàng	to face toward; the aspect or facing of (a building) — 朝 cháo (to face) + 向 xiàng (toward). To face a given way, or the facing itself. Common in describing buildings: 这套房子朝向南 "this flat faces south," a prized aspect for sunlight in Chinese homes.
朝阳	zhāo yáng	the morning sun: the rising sun, dawn light — 朝 zhāo (morning) + 阳 yáng (sun). The sun as it rises, and the fresh light it brings. Used figuratively for anything new and full of promise: 朝阳产业 "a sunrise industry," a sector on the rise.
朝着	cháo zhe	toward; in the direction of (with sustained motion) — 朝 cháo (toward) + 着 zhe (the durative marker). The preposition with a sense of ongoing facing or movement toward something: 朝着大海 "facing the sea," 朝着目标努力 "to work toward a goal." Common in figurative aims as well as physical ones.
嘲笑	cháo xiào	to mock; to ridicule; to jeer at — 嘲 cháo (to taunt; to deride) + 笑 xiào (laugh). To laugh at someone with scorn. This is the cruel edge of 笑: the same sound aimed downward at a target. Takes a direct object, the person mocked.
炒	chǎo	to stir-fry; the wok toss
炒蛋	chǎo dàn	scrambled eggs — 炒 (chǎo, to stir-fry) + 蛋: eggs broken into a hot wok and pushed around until they set in soft curds. The same 炒 powers most Chinese cooking, so 炒蛋 reads as plainly as English "scrambled eggs."
车	chē	vehicle; car; train
车次	chē cì	train number — G-trains are the fastest, D-trains next. 检票口 (jiǎnpiàokǒu) is the boarding gate, shown on the screens next to the train number; 车厢 (chēxiāng) is the carriage, 座位号 the seat number.
车间	chē jiān	workshop, shop floor — Named by process: 冲压车间 stamping, 焊接车间 welding, 注塑车间 injection molding, 喷涂车间 painting, 组装车间 assembly, 包装车间 packing. 无尘车间 (wúchén chējiān) is a clean room.
车票	chē piào	travel ticket: a ticket for a bus, train, or coach — 车 chē (vehicle; car; train) + 票 piào (slip; ticket). A ticket for ground transport: a bus ticket, a train ticket, a coach ticket. The slip that proves you have paid your fare and may board. By the same pattern, 机票 jīpiào is a plane ticket and 船票 chuánpiào is a boat ticket.
车同轨	chē tóng guǐ	carts made to the same track — Qin standardized the gauge of cart axles so that all wheeled vehicles fit the same ruts. The empire-wide road system, including the 直道 (zhídào, "straight roads") radiating from the capital Xianyang, depended on this.
车站	chē zhàn	station; bus or train stop — 车 (chē, vehicle) + 站 (stop): the place where buses or trains halt and passengers board. The base word for any ground-transit station; specify with 火车站 huǒchēzhàn (train station) or 汽车站 qìchēzhàn (bus station).
沉	chén	heavy; sunken
陈旧	chén jiù	outdated; obsolete; outmoded — 陈 (chén, to lay out; stale, old , grain stored too long; also the Chen state of the Spring and Autumn period) + 旧 (old). Old in the sense of having overstayed its usefulness: 陈旧的观念 (outdated concepts), 陈旧的设备 (obsolete equipment).
趁	chèn	taking advantage of; while
称	chēng	To weigh — To weigh. 称一下 ("weigh it, please") hands the fruit to the vendor. The same character read chèn means "to fit," as in 称心如意, suiting one's heart's wishes.
称呼	chēng hū	form of address
称谓	chēng wèi	Form of address, title, mode of address , the technical term for the entire system of address terms. 亲属称谓 (qīnshǔ chēngwèi) refers specifically to kinship terms. Understanding 称谓 is considered a basic social competency.
撑	chēng	stuffed, overfull
成本	chéng běn	cost — What the factory pays, as opposed to 价格 (jiàgé), what you pay. 原材料 (yuáncáiliào, raw materials) and 人工 (réngōng, labour) are the two cost lines you will hear cited when a price goes up. 利润 (lìrùn) is profit.
成都	Chéng dū	Chengdu city
成功	chéng gōng	success: to succeed — 成 chéng (to accomplish; to become) + 功 gōng (achievement). Literally "to complete the achievement." Both verb (to succeed) and noun (success). One of the most common words in modern Chinese, the natural opposite of 失败 shībai (failure). 成功人士 = a successful person. 取得成功 = to attain success.
成绩	chéng jì	grades; results; achievement (in study or work) — 成 chéng (accomplished; finished) + 绩 jì (achievement; spun work). The measured outcome of effort, most often school marks: 考试成绩 "exam results," 成绩单 "a report card." The finished score that shows how the work turned out.
成家	chéng jiā	to start a family; to marry and establish a household — 成 chéng (to become; to accomplish; to complete) + 家 jiā. The moment a household comes into being, through marriage and setting up a home together. One of the defining life-stage transitions in Chinese culture, paired with 立业 (establish a career) in the famous set phrase 成家立业.
成就	chéng jiù	achievement; accomplishment — 成 chéng (accomplished) + 就 jiù (to attain). The lasting result of effort, the achievement one can point to: 巨大的成就 "a great achievement," 学术成就 "academic accomplishments." Weightier and more public than a single completed task.
成熟	chéng shú	mature; ripe; well-developed — 成 chéng (fully formed) + 熟 shú (ripe; cooked). Ripe in the literal sense for fruit and crops, and mature in the figurative sense for people, plans, and technologies: 成熟的苹果 "a ripe apple," 想法还不成熟 "the idea is not yet fully developed."
成为	chéng wéi	to become — 成 chéng (to accomplish; to become; to complete) + 为 wéi (to be; to serve as). The standard verb for transformation into a new state or role. Followed directly by a noun: 成为医生 (to become a doctor), 成为朋友 (to become friends), 成为问题 (to become a problem).
成语	chéng yǔ	chengyu — four-character set phrases — 成 chéng (to become; set; established) + 语 yǔ (speech; phrase). Set phrases , the four-character classical idioms that are one of the most distinctive features of Chinese. 成语 are compressed narratives, mostly four characters, encoding a story from history or classical literature.
成长	chéng zhǎng	to grow up; to mature; personal development — 成 chéng (to become; to complete) + 长 zhǎng (to grow). The compound for the full arc of development: physical, psychological, and social maturation. 成长故事 = coming-of-age story. 成长经历 = formative experiences. Also used for adult development: 这段经历让我很成长 "this experience helped me grow a lot."
承担	chéng dān	to bear; take responsibility
承认	chéng rèn	to admit, to acknowledge, to recognize formally — 承 chéng (to bear; to receive; to assume) + 认 rèn. To shoulder the recognition of something , a fact, a fault, a state's legitimacy. Spans personal admission ("I admit I was wrong") and formal recognition (one country recognizing another diplomatically).
诚	chéng	sincerity; genuine
诚实	chéng shí	honest
诚信	chéng xìn	integrity; honesty and trustworthiness; good faith — 诚 chéng (sincerity; genuine; true to one's heart , 诚 is itself 言 + 成 chéng, words that have been completed, words that have come to pass; sincerity as the alignment of word with reality) + 信 xìn (trustworthiness).
城堡	chéng bǎo	castle; fortress — 城 chéng (wall; fortified place) + 堡 bǎo (fort; stronghold). A castle or fortress, the walled stronghold of a lord. 城堡 is the standard translation for the European castle, and the word most readers meet first in fairy tales and fantasy.
城隍	Chéng huáng	the City God — divine magistrate of the underworld — 城隍 (lit. "city wall and moat") is the divine magistrate of a city or county, presiding over both the living city and its deceased residents in the underworld. He holds court in his temple (城隍庙 Chénghuáng miào), trying the souls of the recently dead to assign their afterlife fate.
城隍庙	Chéng huáng miào	City God Temple
城门	chéng mén	city gate — 城 chéng (city; wall) + 门 mén (gate; door). The controlled opening in the wall, the single point where the inside and outside of a city legally met. City gates were closed at night, manned by guards, and named individually; a walled capital might have a dozen, each a landmark in its own right.
城墙	chéng qiáng	city wall; rampart — 城 chéng (city; wall) + 墙 qiáng (wall). The original sense of 城 made explicit. Where 城 alone once meant the wall, modern Mandarin pairs it with 墙 to specify the physical structure as opposed to the city it encloses.
城区	chéng qū	urban district
城市	chéng shì	city — 城 chéng (walled settlement) + 市 shì (market). The most ordinary modern word for "city," and a fossil of how cities formed: a wall (城) for defense, a market (市) for exchange. The two together make the thing.
城镇	chéng zhèn	towns and cities; urban areas — 城 chéng (city) + 镇 zhèn (town). A collective term spanning the whole range of urban settlement, from large cities down to market towns, used especially in contrast to the countryside (农村 nóngcūn).
程度	chéng dù	degree, extent
程度补语	chéng dù bǔ yǔ	degree complement
吃饱	chī bǎo	to eat one's fill — 吃 (chī, eat) + 饱 (full) as a resultative pair: to eat until satisfied. The negative is 没吃饱 (méi chī bǎo, did not eat enough), and a question often takes 吃饱了吗 (chī bǎo le ma).
吃醋	chī cù	to be jealous (in love); to eat vinegar — 吃 chī + 醋 cù (vinegar). Eating vinegar: romantic jealousy. The origin story: a Tang dynasty concubine had to drink vinegar as punishment when she defied orders, giving rise to this vivid metaphor. Exclusively romantic jealousy (not professional envy, which is 嫉妒 jídù).
吃饭	chī fàn	To eat a meal , literally "eat cooked grain — To eat a meal , literally "eat cooked grain." Used as a general term for eating any meal regardless of whether rice is served. 吃 means "to eat"; 饭 means cooked grain or, by extension, any meal.
吃苦	chī kǔ	to endure hardship; to eat bitterness — 吃 chī (eat) + 苦 kǔ (bitter; hardship). To eat bitterness , to endure difficulty without complaint. One of the most culturally loaded compounds in Chinese. 能吃苦 (able to endure hardship) is a major virtue. 吃苦耐劳 = endure hardship and labor without complaint.
吃亏	chī kuī	to suffer a loss; to be at a disadvantage; to get the short end — 吃 chī + 亏 kuī (deficit; shortfall). To eat a shortfall , to come out worse, to be cheated or disadvantaged. 吃亏是福 chī kuī shì fú (suffering loss is a blessing) is a common proverb: the Daoist wisdom that accepting disadvantage builds character.
吃了	chī le	ate; has eaten (the action is complete) — The most basic post-verb 了 construction. The 了 immediately follows the verb and signals that the action was completed. With a bare monosyllabic object, the 了 sits between verb and object. With a quantified object, this placement is even clearer and more natural.
吃了吗	chī le ma	have you eaten
吃人	chī rén	Eating people, man-eating , Lu Xun term for the cannibalistic logic he claimed to find underlying Confucian social morality.
吃完饭就睡	chī wán fàn jiù shuì	sleeps right after eating (immediately, habitual) — 就 in habitual statements describes characteristic immediate sequences , what this person always does right after something else. Often used with mild commentary (it might be a bad habit, or an endearing one). The 就 here encodes: no delay at all between finishing dinner and falling asleep.
吃香	chī xiāng	to be popular, sought-after
池	chí	pond
迟缓	chí huǎn	sluggish, tardy
持有	chí yǒu	to hold (a position, certificate)
尺寸	chǐ cùn	dimensions, size — Named after two old units of length, 尺 and 寸. 外径 (wàijìng) is outer diameter, 内径 (nèijìng) inner diameter, 壁厚 (bìhòu) wall thickness, 高度 height.
赤	chì	deep crimson; bare
赤壁之战	Chì bì zhī Zhàn	the Battle of Red Cliffs
赤脚	chì jiǎo	barefoot — 赤 chì (red; bare; naked) + 脚. Bare-footed. Famously the term in 赤脚医生 chìjiǎo yīshēng, the "barefoot doctors" of the Mao-era rural healthcare program , village paramedics with minimal training who made primary care available across the countryside.
充电	chōng diàn	to charge (device)
充分	chōng fèn	ample; full; sufficient — 充 chōng (to fill) + 分 fèn (measure; share). Filled to the proper measure, fully sufficient: 充分的准备 "ample preparation," 充分利用 "to make full use of." Note the fèn reading.
充实	chōng shí	substantial; enriched; full (of a life or content) — 充 chōng (to fill) + 实 shí (full; substantial). Filled to fullness, with nothing hollow: 生活很充实 "a full, rewarding life." As a verb it means to enrich or strengthen: 充实内容 "to flesh out the content."
充足	chōng zú	ample; abundant; fully sufficient — 充 (chōng, to fill; to be full) + 足 (sufficient). Filled to sufficiency , not merely enough but abundantly enough. 阳光充足 (ample sunlight); 准备充足 (fully prepared); 充足的理由 (compelling reasons). Stronger than 足够 zúgòu (merely enough).
重庆小面	Chóng qìng xiǎo miàn	Chongqing Spicy Noodles
崇祯帝	Chóng zhēn Dì	last Ming emperor
宠物	chǒng wù	pet
绸	chóu	silk fabric
